# PROLOGUE.

A soft voice calls me back
To <term>Pindus<note>original: "Pinde." A mountain range in Greece, traditionally considered the home of the Muses; here, it symbolizes the realm of poetry and artistic inspiration.</note></term> which I had left;
And it is the voice of beauty:
Obedient, I return there for her.
Will my return be a happy one?
Of this mountain which deceives the eye,
The peak, already lost in the clouds,
Rises still from day to day.
Let us try. *If poetry*
*Invents and lives on fiction,*
*It lives, above all, on action:*
Away, then, with the new heresy <note>The author likely refers to a contemporary literary movement that prioritized abstract thought over narrative "action."</note>.
But I want fresh colors;
I want smiles and tears.
<term>Laura's<note>Likely an allusion to the muse of the Italian poet Petrarch, used here to represent a sophisticated female guide or patroness.</note></term> counsel guides me;
Even before mine, her quick intuition
Saw flowers within my subject.
More beautiful than my beautiful <term>Isaure<note>original: "Isaure." A reference to Clémence Isaure, the legendary medieval patroness of the "Floral Games" (Jeux Floraux), a famous poetic competition in Toulouse.</note></term>,

<summary>In this poetic prologue, the author announces their return to writing, inspired by a beautiful muse and guided by the advice of a figure named Laura. The text emphasizes that poetry should be rooted in action and emotion rather than mere abstract fiction.</summary>
<keywords>prologue, Pindus, Muses, poetry, Laura, Clémence Isaure, literary theory, inspiration</keywords>

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

## A POEM.

---

### CANTO THE FIRST.

Solemn <term>Clio<note>The Muse of History in Greek mythology, often depicted with a scroll or tablets.</note></term>, what do your annals offer me?
Long discords, rival storms,
Ambition shaking the foundations of states,
Here tears, there the song of combat;
The endless history of conquerors,
Unjust force, and laurels without glory.
From time to time, there shine within this chaos
A few good kings, the support of the weak,
A few pure days, a gift from wisdom,
Which new storms shall surely follow.
Clio, often, in the face of your tiring pride,
We prefer the grace of your rival <note>The author likely refers to Erato (the Muse of lyric or love poetry) or Thalia (the Muse of idylls and comedy), suggesting a desire to mix historical facts with imaginative storytelling.</note>.
You are sisters; keep then your own features,
And allow her to cheer their sadness.
I shall dare it; my hand upon your <term>cypresses<note>The cypress tree is a traditional symbol of mourning and death, fitting for the "sadness" of historical records mentioned by the author.</note></term>

<summary>The author invokes Clio, the Muse of History, lamenting that human records are filled primarily with war and suffering. He asks permission to temper the grim reality of history with the grace of her sister Muses to create a more pleasing narrative.</summary>
<keywords>Clio, Muse, History, Rosicrucians, epic poetry, war, peace, poetic inspiration</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical invocation of the Muse Clio to the specific narrative setting of the poem. The author moves from general historical lamentation to the specific figures—real and legendary—who will ground the story of the Rosicrucians.</meta>

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

Will know how to scatter the roses of <term>Permessus<note>A river in Boeotia, Greece, sacred to the Muses. To "scatter the roses of Permessus" means to write inspired, graceful poetry.</note></term>.
No lessons; we are taught enough and too much,
For us, flowers are worth more than fruits.
Here are flowers—that is to say, women,
Flowers of pleasure and even of reason.
If the Frenchman still deserves this name,
He loves, honors, and shall sing of the ladies.
O indulgent sex, for you my new verse
Exhumes the cradle of the <term>Rosicrucians<note>A mystical secret society (the Order of the Rose Cross) that claimed to possess secret wisdom passed down from antiquity. The author is here proposing a poetic origin for the group.</note></term>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Over the English reigned the wise Elfride.
Daughter of Edgar, whose swift valor
Thrice conquered Scotland and the Welsh,
Crowned wife of <term>Chérébert<note>Likely referring to Charibert I (c. 517–567), a Merovingian King of Paris. The poet is blending historical periods, as Edgar the Peaceful lived in the 10th century.</note></term>,
And brought in triumph into Paris,
She shone within the palace of kings.
Soon a mother, France with love
Watched the childhood of her daughters grow.
But Chérébert, but Edgar and his son,
In twenty battles followed by twenty more,
Seeking, finding, exhausting victory,
Bloodied a phantom of glory:
They have fallen; no tears for them.

<summary>The poet shifts focus from the heavy annals of history to a lighter, romanticized narrative centered on women and the legendary origins of the Rosicrucians. He introduces Queen Elfride, a figure connecting the English and French thrones, set against a backdrop of ceaseless, bloody male-led warfare.</summary>
<keywords>Permessus, Rosicrucians, Elfride, Edgar the Peaceful, Charibert I, Paris, chivalry, poetic inspiration</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions into the first Canto of the poem, describing the peaceful and prosperous reign of Queen Elfride in London. It follows the introduction of Elfride as the daughter of Edgar and wife of Chérébert, setting a scene of courtly splendor and political stability.</meta>

### CANTO I.

->Elfride reigns, and the English are happy.<-

The month was being born when the earth blooms again,
A month of gaiety, of hope, and of love <note>The poet is referring to the month of May, traditionally the season of courtly festivals and the "rebirth" of the natural world.</note>.
From the great lords then, the voluntary homage
Of the sovereign came to adorn the court.
All displayed their hereditary splendor.
Dukes and barons, <term>bannerets<note>A rank of knighthood; a knight who was entitled to lead a company of men under his own banner.</note></term>, lords of the manor,
Fill London and behold its festivities,
The gifts, the arts of distant kingdoms,
The peaceful conquests of beauty,
The brilliance of the people, and their joyful feasts.
Within the palace, their crowd ceaselessly
Spreads, circulates, or returns or flows away.
Everywhere are games, reviving pleasures,
Games without turmoil and which Elfride shares.
To her charms, six <term>lustres<note>A "lustre" is a Roman unit of time representing a five-year period. At "six lustres and three years," the Queen is described as being thirty-three years old.</note></term> and three years
Still leave the roses of her youth,
And even without a crown she would obtain the homage
That duty commands from her subjects.
Dunstan and Engist <note>Dunstan refers to Saint Dunstan (c. 909–988), the influential Archbishop of Canterbury. Engist refers to Hengist, the legendary Saxon invader. The poet is mixing historical figures from different centuries to create a legendary British past.</note> extend her power:
Equal to kings, yet submissive in her presence,
Love increases their faithful devotion,

And in their hearts is a credulous hope.
Young Emma mirrors the Queen’s
Majesty, sweetness, and grace;
But her gaze, at times languid <note>In 19th-century romantic poetry, a "languid" or "languishing" gaze often suggested a sensitive, soulful, or melancholic disposition, making a character more sympathetic to the reader.</note>,
Gives her features a more touching charm.
Blanche, her sister—the lighthearted and lively Blanche—
Pleases in a different way, yet always knows how to please:
Her heart deceives her, and her fearful pride
Vainly believes it can escape from love's grasp.
Rich and powerful, the cold Oswal and the proud Althor
In vain offer the bonds of marriage <note>original: "hymen... la chaîne"</note> to the Queen’s daughters;
And, having no rivals, they continue to hope.
Others shone within this crowded court.
But by their features, and by their effortless grace,
The eye easily recognizes the Frenchmen,
Who were then the voluntary subjects of Elfride <note>This suggests a legendary or idealized history where French nobles choose to serve the English crown out of admiration rather than conquest.</note>.
Raoul and Albert, pages to the two princesses,
Have earned the royal favor:
Since childhood, to their noble mistresses
They have been devoted, and they grow in the pursuit of honor.
Even more beautiful, their sister, standing beside Elfride

### CANTO I.

Was raised, and always follows her steps:
Indifferent, Isaure does not feel
What she inspires, and her timid virtue
Extinguishes the incense offered to her charms <note>original: "appas", a poetic term for beauty or physical attractions</note>.
Charles and Roger have also from France
Left the court, its peaceful opulence:
Then faithful squires to Elfride,
Both shone in the warriors' games <note>This refers to tournaments or martial exercises common in chivalric life</note>.
Julius and Raymond, less constant in their attendance near her,
Far from the city and dear to their vassals <term>vassals: feudal subjects who held land in exchange for loyalty and service to a lord</term>,
Embellished their new estates:
To London, finally, duty recalls them.

Near this river <note>The River Thames, portrayed here as a burgeoning center of global commerce</note> where today float
A thousand vessels and the treasures of the world,
In the green meadows enamored of its waves,
Upon the path that winds along with it,
Elfride and her noble procession march.
On horses as white as snow,
Light and lively, yet docile to the bit,
And guided at will by a delicate hand,
Joy and fresh graces shine
From these beauties, faithful to custom <note>original: "à l'usage fidelles", suggesting they are adhering to the expected traditions and etiquette of their rank</note>,

### THE ROSICRUCIANS,

Who, carrying sparrowhawks <note>original: "éperviers"; these small birds of prey were traditionally used by the nobility for hunting smaller birds like quails or larks</note> upon their fists,
Release these warrior-birds into flight.
Each one takes wing, and with a rapid wing
Pursues from afar either the timid quail,
Or the lark of the morning concerts
Whom fear leads astray in the air:
He seizes it, hears the cry of joy,
Toward his mistress he flies back uncertainly,
And generous with regret, into her hand
Drops the quivering prey.
   But pity, which of a charming sex
Is the truest, the most noble ornament,
Already pronounces a furtive pardon;
And the lark, captive for but a single moment,
Finds once again its sweet liberty,
Its azure sky, its plaintive companion,
And the brilliant gaiety of its song.
   In the disorder and noise of the hunt
Love seizes a few happy moments.
Near Edgitha, Roger finally takes his place.
He asks for the bonds of a prompt marriage <term>hymen: a poetic term for marriage, named after the Greek god of wedding ceremonies</term>.
It is with regret that Edgitha defers them;

### THE ROSICRUCIANS,

And of Crodo <note>A Saxon deity often depicted standing on a fish and carrying a basket of flowers; here identified as a brother of Odin</note>, one of the brothers of Odin,
Deep in the woods they have hidden the image.
There, one after another, by various paths,
During the night the faithful arrive.
To the lying god they offer songs,
A foolish incense, fresh garlands.
Fantasmagor <note>A name likely derived from "phantasmagoria," here personified as a "perverse angel" or demon who deceives the worshippers</note>, one of the perverse angels,
Hears their vows: to their mute idol
From time to time he lends his voice;
To revive a languishing hope,
He promises them empire and riches;
And it is even said that to his young priestesses
Sometimes he grants a magical power.
The zeal of the three friends <note>Referring to Alkent, Rénisthal, and Odon mentioned on the previous page</note> secretly
Still supports these deceitful altars.
In a court unfaithful to their gods,
Their embarrassment, their grief betrays them;
And near Elfride they seem like strangers.
    The sound of the fife announces another hunt.
From its reeds, threatened by a noisy dog,
High into the air rises the heron,
The intrepid falcon launched upon it...

### CANTO I.

He sets off, and the shouts embolden his daring.
Circling, he climbs toward the heavens;
Swift and proud, he reaches, he overtakes
The ambitious flight of the enemy,
Attacks him at last, while in the clouds
His lucky escape was vanishing from sight.
Despite his strength, for the valorous falcon
Skill is necessary in this combat.
Struck twenty times by a vigorous heel <note>A poetic reference to the falcon's "stoop" or the strike of its talons</note>,
The fishing bird <note>original: "l'oiseau pécheur"; referring to the heron mentioned on the previous page</note> descends back toward the earth.
If he has lost the sanctuary of the reeds,
He can still plunge into the waters.
Constantly struck, and directing his flight,
His flight finally plunges toward the wave.
But the falcon's skill forestalls him,
And above the waves his talon holds him back.
Victorious then, he climbs again, he pauses,
And for a moment motionless in the air,
His fixed eyes proudly ask
If he should keep or yield his conquest.
The sweet flute <note>A signal from the falconer to the bird to return or release</note> announces the pardon:
The noble bird immediately releases his prey,

The aged trunks and the young elms.
Alkent foresees its detours, outstrips it,
And near an oak, he takes his place in silence.
The dart, launched by his robust hand,
Reaches the monster's flank; suddenly,
Turning about, the beast rushes upon him.
With a nimble leap, the clever hunter evades it,
And strikes again at the already bleeding flank.
The bull falls, and promptly rises again.
Tremble, Alkent, flee! Even as he retreats,
He sets his sword against that broad forehead. <term>sword: The original "glaive" refers to a heavy, double-edged blade, often associated with heroic or classical combat.</term>
A deceptive success! Driven into the skull,
The iron snaps: with trembling hands,
Alkent seizes the menacing horns,
He wrestles, fights, pushes, and is pushed back;
He evades and exhausts the monster's fury,
Then, summoning back his failing strength,
The bull is finally cast down upon the grass.
For the hunters, this fall is cause for celebration.
The triumphant Alkent stands still for a moment,
Catches his breath, and proud of his conquest,

### CANTO I.

To finish it off, his axe finally severs the enormous head from the palpitating monster. Joyfully he departs, followed by the hunters and surrounded by fluttering banners, howling hounds, and warrior horns; he tastes the sweetness of victory. To these delights, hope adds even more. He marches radiantly toward the procession; suddenly all eyes are fixed upon him; and, ever proud, he casts at the feet of Isaure <note>Isaure appears here as a figure of courtly affection, though her subsequent refusal suggests a strict adherence to social hierarchy.</note> the bloody gift, the most flattering tribute that valor can offer to beauty.

She draws back, confused by the gift, and says: "Your hand is mistaken, I see. To this homage, to this gift which I refuse, the queen alone had rights here." He remains silent at this noble speech, but resentment <note>original: "dépit" — a word signifying a mix of irritation and wounded pride</note> colors his face.

In the valley, one follows the footsteps of Elfride. There, she presides over mock battles. <term>mock battles: Referred to as "factices combats," these were staged tournaments or displays of martial skill where the physical danger was minimized for sport.</term> In that place, the warrior takes up a new weapon, a weapon of peace, without point and without edge.

<summary>Following the hunt, Alkent presents the severed head of the bull to Isaure, who rejects the gift as a breach of protocol, claiming it belongs to the queen. The narrative then shifts to a valley where Elfride oversees a tournament of non-lethal, ceremonial combat.</summary>
<keywords>Alkent, Isaure, Elfride, hunting ritual, courtly etiquette, tournament, mock combat, social hierarchy</keywords>

The Englishman returns, the page whom he threatens

### CANTO I.

What he has said, and above all what he hopes for.
Jule <note>Likely a variation of the name Julius; Jule appears to be the "young hero" or a companion of Raoul mentioned in the previous scene.</note> is cherished, Rénisthal is odious;

From Olside <note>A female character, possibly a lady of the court or Jule’s beloved, whose name suggests a Germanic or Nordic influence common in Romantic literature.</note> one sees only silence and tears;
And her sorrow passes into every heart.
What! does heaven thus take revenge for its own gifts?
Is there never pleasure without bitterness? <note>original: "plaisirs sans mélange" — literally "pleasures without mixture," referring to the idea that pure happiness is always tainted by sorrow.</note>
No; too often the distant cloud,
Whose birth the eye can barely perceive,
Grows slowly, unfolds in silence,
And threatens the end of a perfect day.

## CANTO SECOND.

I love the brilliance of this faithful court
Which the banquet near Elfride <note>A name likely derived from the Old English "Aelfthryth," common in Romantic-era literature set in historical England.</note> calls to mind,
The noble, playful tone of these Frenchmen,
And their flattery, accepted by the ladies. <note>original: "encens" — literally "incense," here used as a metaphor for the sweet words of courtship.</note>
Paris has given London these fineries:
This long hair braided in separate strands,
And entwined with flowers upon the head,
The purple and gold of the light borders,
The grace and curvature of these bare arms,
This breast veiled only by modesty
Where a symbolic cross <note>This "symbolic cross" refers to the emblem of the Rosicrucians, the mysterious secret society that gives the poem its name.</note> floats and shines,
The soft linen of the long tunic,
The shorter linen falling to the knees,
This white cothurne <term>cothurne: an ancient Greek buskin or high-soled boot, often used in literature to signify a tragic or noble classical style</term>, and finally this ancient veil,
Born in Greece and passed down to us.
The beautiful queen, Isaure, and the princesses,
Charm the eyes and leave the choice hanging in the balance. <note>The poet suggests that their beauty is so equal that one cannot easily choose who is most fair.</note>

->### CANTO II.<-

To secret vows, to burgeoning affections,
Others still—they all had a claim to notice.
Raymond alone, indifferent to them all,
Along the woods, beside the peaceful river,
Walks along in his dreaming silence.
But there he sees the lively gaiety of the people,
Their various games, their easy happiness.
Further still he walks, and his plaintive mouth
Finally betrays the trouble in his heart:
“What am I doing here, far from you, dear Aldine?
I will no longer obey your advice.
But tell me, when will your refusals cease?
French, poor, an orphan at fifteen,
Indifferent to my jealous vassals <note>vassals: feudal subjects or subordinates who owe allegiance to a lord—in this case, Raymond.</note>
Who are aware of the sweetest of my vows,
Am I not your necessary support?
A king often crowns a shepherdess; <note>This is a common literary trope where true love transcends social class, often referenced in pastoral poetry and folklore.</note>
We love each other; let there be no more rank between us.
You do not believe my constant promise;
You fear marriage <note>original: "hymen" — a classical reference to Hymenaios, the god of marriage ceremonies, commonly used in 18th and 19th-century poetry to signify a wedding.</note>; you fear from my youth
A regret that would punish our love.
But reason approves my affection.”

<summary>Raymond wanders away from the courtly festivities to reflect on his love for Aldine, a poor orphan of lower social standing. He laments her refusals and argues that their mutual love should overcome the barriers of rank and social expectation.</summary>

Has near him only a son who is still a child;

### CANTO II.

Weeping, they are dragged onto the ship.
Soon they discover the port of Wailte <note>original: "Wailte" — likely a fictionalized location name, possibly a variation of a British place-name used to evoke a medieval setting.</note>.

Young and sensitive, he offers you marriage <note>original: "hyménée" — a poetic term for a wedding or marriage, derived from Hymen, the Greek god of marriage ceremonies.</note>;
Speak, and choose: the throne, or death <note>original: "trépas" — a literary and dramatic term for passing away, often used in tragic poetry.</note>."
She replies: "I am but a simple shepherdess <note>original: "bergère" — in the pastoral tradition, this signifies a young woman of rustic virtue and humble birth.</note>.
The prince would descend to my level in vain:
For I have given my word forever.
I shall die, then; but spare my father."
For you I tremble, O rose of beauty,
Rose of honor and of fidelity!

Her father comes, whose weakened voice
Lets slip broken words:
"A king... marriage... you would save my life,
Yours... alas! I approve of your refusal."
"— No, you shall live, my father; no more alarms.
O vanished dreams of love!"

Disturbs the festivities and approaches Elfride.

## CANTO II.

"Queen," he said, "far to the north upon the waters,
I have recognized the banners of the Danes.
They seek the shores of England.
They are numerous and daring: their vessels,
Against the winds, the tide <note>original: "reflux" — Referring to the ebb of the tide or the backward flow of water against the ships.</note>, and the storm,
Struggled still, scattered upon the waves."

Arthur rises: "O Queen! Here my voice
Must be the interpreter of the wise Olcan <note>Olcan: Likely a reference to a counselor or a druidic-style figure, common in the Romantic literature of this period.</note>.

I have fought beside the King your father.
Daughter of Edgar <note>Edgar the Peaceful: King of the English from 959 to 975. The poem places the Queen as his successor and heir.</note>, love of England,

He speaks, and departs; and the feeling Queen,
Pitying the long suffering of this lover,
Gives a secret sigh to his destiny.

Other thoughts give rise to her sadness.
She had believed that for the faithful English
Her happy reign would preserve the peace:
Jealous fate has deceived her wisdom.

### CANTO II <note>original: "Chant II"</note>

## CANTO THREE

They have tamed the fury of the northern waves,
And they cover the shores of England,
These proud Danes, who, though always driven back,
Always return, hungry for plunder,
To snatch from the arts their ancient heritage,
To tear the gold from overturned temples,
To carry off the opulence of fertile fields,
And to squander in the heart of lawlessness
All the treasures amassed in blood.
Young Harold <note>original: "Harol" — likely a reference to a Danish chieftain or a stylized version of a Viking leader like Harald.</note>, whose noble lineage
Traces back to the gods worshipped in Holstein <note>A historic region in Northern Germany, then under Danish influence; the "gods" refers to the Norse pantheon like Odin and Thor.</note>,
Smiles as he watches their boiling audacity,
And foretells for them a certain victory.
Upon the rock that overlooks the plain
He stands tall, and his sovereign voice
Resounds afar: “Friends, here is the day
That your idle valor has been calling for.”

<summary>Canto III opens with the arrival of Danish invaders on the English coast, led by the young leader Harold. The passage depicts the Vikings as a relentless force motivated by plunder and divine heritage as they prepare for battle.</summary>
<keywords>Danish invasion, Harold, England, Vikings, Holstein, plunder, Norse mythology, Canto III</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of the Danish leader, Harold, as he rallies his forces on the English coast. He justifies the Viking invasion by appealing to the historical precedent of Germanic migrations and the law of the "right of the strongest."</meta>

## CANTO III

Day of hope! Odin <note>The primary god in Norse mythology, associated with war, wisdom, and the guiding of souls.</note> has guided the return of our ships to this shore. Why these cries and these vain insults from the nations where our wandering swords sometimes leave deep wounds? What are we? We are what the Franks and the Clovises <note>Clovis I (c. 466–511) was the first king to unite the Frankish tribes; the poet uses his name here as a plural title for great Germanic conquerors.</note> were: the conquerors of Gaul <note>The Roman province comprising modern-day France and Belgium, which was seized by the Franks.</note>. What shall we do? Exactly what the Angles and Saxons <note>Germanic tribes who invaded and settled in Britain starting in the 5th century, becoming the "English" ancestors whom the Danes are now attacking.</note> did on this island; they who, for the sake of a fertile soil, abandoned their barren <term>avare: literally "miserly" or stingy, describing lands that yield little food</term> deserts. On these shores covered in ruins—through battles, treaties, and vengeance—they established their bloody power. What then would those warrior founders say if they could hear their cowardly heirs? Those who, without shame and even without memory, wither our glory with a shameful name? What rights do they have? They have the right of force; well then, you shall have those same rights, intrepid Danes. Since heaven always ordains it so: that weakness must surrender to strength its fat flocks and their soft fleeces,

<summary>In this passage, the Danish leader Harold justifies his invasion of England by framing it as a historical necessity, comparing the Danes to the original Anglo-Saxon and Frankish conquerors. He argues that since the current inhabitants gained the land through force, they have no moral standing to condemn the Danes for doing the same.</summary>
<keywords>Odin, Franks, Clovis, Gaul, Angles, Saxons, Danish invasion, Viking law, right of conquest, Canto III</keywords>

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

> The joyful wines, the floating gold of the harvests:

> And beauty, the sweetest of treasures. »

While he speaks, sudden cries escaping from the silence applaud his voice; at his final words, the shields struck by swords resound up to the heavens. His hand then shows the crowded ranks <note>original: "phalanges"</note> the plain and allows for rest. The flags are planted upon the rock; tents are pitched in the furrows; and the warrior, though eager for battle, gaily receives a meager <note>original: "indigent," meaning poor or lacking. The soldiers eat sparingly now because they expect to feast on stolen goods later.</note> meal which the abundance of feasts will soon follow. Beside Harold, intoxicated by a youthful hope, walk Eric and Oldar, chiefs under his command—soldiers only recently, and powerful today: to their bravery, to their tall stature, the entire army obeys without a murmur.

### CANTO III.

They were recounting their past triumphs.
At that moment, arriving with hurried steps,
Are Fraull and Ghesler, skillful emissaries,
Who, having been prisoners of the English in the past,
Were educated among them and lived freely in their cities;
They know their customs, their power, and their laws.
"Prince," said Fraull, "Odin <note>The high god of the Norse pantheon, associated here with divine favor for the invasion.</note> favors you.
He joins to your noble enterprise
The malcontents <note>original: "mécontens," referring to locals who are dissatisfied with the current English rule and willing to defect.</note> loyal to Crodo <note>An ancient Saxon deity, sometimes depicted with a wheel or a basket of flowers, representing the pagan roots of some inhabitants who might resist the Christian king.</note>.
They all await the Danish flag.
But scattered, they still remain silent.
If you wish it, with a few soldiers,
Ghesler and I shall go ahead of your steps.
By paths that even the English do not know,
From woods to woods, I can march cautiously,
And approach London without peril."
— "Very well, set out then. And you whom Odin protects,
Brave chiefs, divide the warriors of Norway
Into two parts: by splitting your strikes,
You will obtain a richer pillage;
To the malcontents, promise a share [of the spoils],
And honors: London <note>original: "Londre"</note> is the rendezvous."

> "Leave tender and weak childhood in peace;

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In Harol’s camp were two young brothers,
Bold navigators, successful warriors,
And already feared on foreign shores.
Everything is shared between them, even their laurels <note>A classical reference to military honors and victory wreaths.</note>.

### CANTO III.

"Stay united, and you will always conquer."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Already, the frightened weakness of the laborer <note>A poetic way of describing the defenseless farmers and peasants.</note> fled before the triple army. One climbed the heights of the lofty mountains; another sought the depths of the woods; some ran to deep caverns; others, without skill, entrusted themselves to the waves. But sometimes straying from the banner, fierce Danes <note>The Scandinavian invaders under Harol's command.</note> wandered afar, alone or leading a daring troop. Everywhere there was iron, chains, and fire. Everywhere, yielding to sudden assaults, the pride of the ancient battlements <note>original: "antiques créneaux," referring to the crenelated walls of old fortresses or castles.</note> fell.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Further away, Raymond is more tranquil. "Your country claims all its arms," Aldine said to him, "and you do not run to join them?"
— "No, in these places my presence is useful; I will stay."
— "What! Does honor speak in vain?"
— "Honor approves of me."
— "And who, then, stops you?"
— "You alone."
— "Oh heavens!"
— "Yes, the storm of war may finally extend as far as here."
— "Well, what does it matter? And love today..."

Aldine challenges Raymond’s honor for not fighting, only for him to reveal that his devotion to her safety keeps him from the battlefield.</summary>

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

"— It is duty; it is gratitude. From the wise Almon, the father of my childhood, his daughter must find a protector in me.
— If I have any power over your heart, Raymond..."
— "Raymond is no longer listening to you; and advice here is superfluous."

For a long time silent at this strange refusal, Aldine muses; she pities the sadness and the secret torment of her young lover. Then she goes out and changes her attire. Upon her brow, she receives the modest cap of a messenger and a simple page <note>original: "varlet," which in this context refers to a young servant or squire.</note> adorned with a feather; and beneath the coat of arms <note>original: "cotte-d'armes," a surcoat or tunic worn over armor or travel gear.</note>, the curves of her charms disappeared.

Carrying her lute as it swung upon her shoulder, she returns: "Glory awaits you," she said; "go, and I follow you."
— "Dear Aldine!"
— "I am Aldin." <note>She adopts a masculine version of her name to maintain her disguise as a male youth.</note>
— "Dear Aldin, what a blessing! For it truly is one; my pride was blushing..." <note>Raymond felt ashamed to stay behind while others fought, but his love for Aldine prevented him from leaving her; her disguise resolves his shame.</note>
— "My eyes have read your sorrowful soul. Let us depart together; and above all, forget the language of a lover until our return."

<summary>Aldine resolves Raymond's conflict between his military duty and his protective love for her by disguising herself as a male page named Aldin. This allows them to join the war effort together, preserving Raymond's honor while ensuring they remain side-by-side.</summary>

### CANTO III.

"— Rest assured; silent and wise,
Love... — Swear it. — Well then, I give my oath."

More unhappy still, Jule remained unaware of the recurring fear felt by the weeping Olfide. Deaf to her refusals, the hateful Rénisthal hurries the fatal day of his marriage <term>hymen: a poetic reference to the wedding bond, often used in literature to signify a marriage rite</term>. Odon remains silent; but his brow is severe. 

Beneath the scattered groves in the garden, his niece, wandering and solitary, often went to hide her timid sorrow; and there at least her tears flow without fear; there her sweet lament rises toward heaven, and she freely invokes her God. 

Sadder still today, in this place she was forgetting the already approaching night. The sudden presence of her lover makes her tremble: "O Jule, what do you dare? Everywhere here, jealous eyes are watching."

"— No, my love is prudent; from the hunt the distant sound has reached me; your uncle follows the trail of a boar; the shadow of the wood covers us; fear no more."

### CANTO III.

“Be prudent, Jule, and preserve our lives.”
Thus spoke her simple fears,
For her husband <note>original: "époux" — though not legally wed, they consider themselves married by their private vow</note> thus her tears flow,
And this husband groans, makes no reply,
And tenderly presses her in his arms.
Just heaven, helpful to those in misfortune,
Had received their lovers’ oaths <term>sermens: solemn vows of fidelity, often carrying a sacred weight</term>;
Night lends them a favorable veil;
The entire world vanishes for them.
Stay the speed of your flight,
Hours of marriage <term>hymen: a poetic reference to the Greek god of marriage, signifying their union</term>, of happiness, and of ecstasy.
But a muffled sound rises from the depths of the woods;
And they hear the voice of the hunters.
Into the castle returns the beautiful Olfide.
Jule follows her with his eyes, and less timid,
From these gardens he moves away with regret.
With love he burns and for love he sighs.
The sound of the horn <term>cor: a hunting horn used to coordinate groups in the forest</term>, which suddenly fades,
Does not warn his slow and distracted foot.
Rénisthal’s blade is raised against him;
By the hunters soon surrounded,
He defends himself, he strikes, he is struck;

“Be prudent, Jule, and preserve our days.”

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

His helmet falls; in his hands his sword
Breaks; he flees through twists and turns;
He escapes the assassins in the shadows.
But no path through this vast and dark wood
Broke the dense thicket; and for the light of day,
At the foot of a tree, he awaits the return.
His blood flowed; he rises with effort;
A woodcutter comes to lend him his arm,
Recognizes him, and supporting his steps,
Slowly leads him back toward his castle.
Anxiety increases his grief.
That same day to the neighboring village
His command sends a faithful servant
Whose prudence and zeal he knows.
This man flies, and returns in the night:
"Lord," he says, "the news I bring is ill.
Odon <note>Olfide's guardian or father figure mentioned in previous cantos</note>, his niece, and Rénisthal as well,
Are already far away. — Where are they going? — No one knows."
Sad and wavering in his new thoughts,
At last he goes out, he arms his vassals <term>vassaux: in the feudal system, these were men who held land from a lord and were bound to provide military service in return</term>.
Everyone foresaw this necessary departure.
At every moment, the war-cry <term>cri de guerre: a distinctive shout or motto used by soldiers in battle to recognize one another and rally their spirits</term> grew.

### CANTO III.

The sonorous bronze <term>airain: literally "bronze," referring here to the metallic sound of trumpets or horns</term> and the piercing fife,
And the resounding blast of the clarion,
Have replaced the rustic bagpipes <term>musettes: a small bagpipe popular in pastoral music, representing peaceful country life</term>.
The voice of the leaders gathers the soldiers.
In those ancient centuries, they did not know
The art of diminishing the danger of combat.
The brave man then had only his audacity,
And frankly faced death.
No hauberk <term>hautbert: a long shirt of chainmail armor</term>, no arm-guards, no cuirass,
No armor at all, in fact: [only] a light shield,
The sharp lance and the cutting axe,
A half-helmet and its floating plume,
A short vest shining with gold or steel,
A belt where the sword <note>original: "glaive"</note> is attached,
A flexible glove, narrow trousers,
Buskins <term>brodequins: sturdy, high-laced boots</term> or supple boots
Which the stinging spur always arms.
Under this dress, young heroines
Sometimes sought distant enemies,
Or repelled audacious friends.
Of the young Harol, the sister younger still,
The beautiful Osla, triumphs on the field of honor.

<summary>The poem describes the shift from peaceful pastoral life to the preparations for war, detailing the light and decorative armor worn by warriors of this era. It concludes by introducing Osla, a young heroine and sister of Harol, who distinguishes herself in battle.</summary>

### CANTO IV.

“Follow Raoul, begin your glory,

Afflicts the young page’s heart less;
This heart, at last, was opening to hope.

And having secretly reached the outskirts of London,

->### CANTO IV.<-

5.

Modesty’s awakening is swift.
The proud Osla suddenly rises,
Blushing, she takes up her sword <note>original: "glaive" — a poetic term for a sword, heightening her image as a warrior maiden</note> from the grass,
And moves to strike the audacious youth.

"Defend your life!" — "I surrender it to you."

That a fleeing band of laborers <note>original: "laboureurs" — specifically tenant farmers or plowmen, representing the innocent victims of the war</note>

Go to accomplish the holy pilgrimage.

### CANTO V.

"So said Oldar," add the English;
"A province and Blanche are enough for me;
And as a bonus to his rich booty,
Eric shall have the hand of the beautiful Emma.
At least so our skalds <term>skalds: Norse poets and historians who composed and recited epic verses about heroes and gods</term> predict."

Eric, however, halted by Oswal,
Harasses his immobility in vain.
Between them flowed a wide river.
The Danish camp covers the entire plain.
On the other bank, the Englishman, master of the bridge,
Is without a plan; but a deep marsh
Defends his right wing, halfway deployed,
And against a wood his left is supported.
For a long time, Oswal, firm in this position,
Forbids his men from an uncertain attack,
And refusing combat in the plain,
Repels the enemy from the narrow bridge. <note>This describes a tactical stalemate where the English use the terrain—the marsh, the woods, and the bridge—to prevent a full-scale battle against the larger Danish force.</note>
Suddenly, this combat begins.
Paul and Jenny, children of the two brothers,
Whose happy age was nearing fifteen years,
Future spouses, taken from the same village,
Were then captives among the Danes.

Do you hear the harsh and long murmur

## SIXTH CANTO.

Before Harol appears the young Isaure.
Upon her he fixes a curious gaze.
Proud and modest, she lowers her eyes,
And a few tears embellish her still.
He contemplated her features so delicate,
Her serene brow, that charm of innocence,
The strange elegance of her clothing,
That noble bearing, and 그 sweet bashfulness <note>original: "doux embarras"; this suggests a modest or shy discomfort rather than true distress.</note>.
"Thus wills inexorable war,"
He said at last; "but I pity your sorrows.
My soul is incapable of cruelty.
I must, I wish to spare you further tears.
Be in my camp a prisoner without chains,
And fear not there the license of the warriors <note>original: "licence guerrière"; a reference to the historical reality of soldiers' potential for lawless or predatory behavior during wartime.</note>.
You shall soon know that the Dane,
That Harol especially, like your Rosicrucians <term>Rosecroix (Rosicrucians): A legendary spiritual and cultural movement originating in the early 17th century, often associated with secret wisdom, alchemy, and humanitarianism.</term>,

# 84

# LES ROSECROIX,
<note>The Rosicrucians</note>

She wept; and far from her, her brothers
Search for her in vain from two sides.
Elfride too, from moment to moment,
Addresses to heaven even more bitter laments.
Her broken heart curses ambition
And the mad passion for laurels <note>original: "lauriers"; a classical symbol of military glory and victory, here criticized as a hollow pursuit that leads only to suffering.</note>.
But the grief that makes her tears flow
Is soon increased by public alarms.
"The young Harol's valor is praised,"
She said to herself, "and even his prudence.
From the conquered north, he advances toward these walls.
Regulating his march, and a victor without battle,
He restrains the lawlessness <note>original: "la licence"; referring to the lack of restraint, looting, or predatory behavior typical of undisciplined soldiers.</note> of his warriors.
Though numerous, Engist and his soldiers
Do not reassure me in this danger.
Will villagers, peaceful townspeople,
Suddenly cast into the turmoil of camps,
Withstand the shock of these brigands <note>original: "brigands"; a term used here by the besieged to describe the Danish invaders as lawless marauders, despite Harol’s efforts to maintain order.</note>
Who are always armed and always invincible?
Engist’s voice will fire their hearts;
They fear the devastation of their homes;
They will fight, and even with courage;"

<summary>Elfride laments the disappearance of Isaure and the destructive nature of military ambition while watching the disciplined Danish army under Harol approach. She expresses deep doubt that Engist’s militia of ordinary citizens can withstand a professional invading force, despite their bravery and motivation to defend their homes.</summary>

### CANTO VI.

8

"But a single day does not make victors."

->Thus Elfride read into the future.<-

The rising dawn illuminates this combat.
One sees Harol’s youth and brilliance,
And a valor that is wisely intrepid.
One sees Engist, both general and soldier,
Whose strong voice encourages or threatens,
Exhorts, instructs, and whose fortunate audacity
Immediately joins example to the lesson.
Before his blows, Lathmon presents himself.
He loves Enna, who remains ever indifferent,
Always wandering alone in the forests;
She, beautiful and proud, and the widow of a hero,
Refused any new bonds <note>original: "liens nouveaux"; a poetic way of referring to remarriage or new romantic commitments.</note>.
But the cry of battle has made itself heard;
Then, prompt to descend from the distant mountain,

Lathmon beside her and his numerous rivals
Are assembled, and their vain prayer
Demands a choice that is not in her heart.

"Shall be mine." Her word is sacred.

His hand, severed by a swifter sword,
Falls upon the grass, where trembling and pale
It still grips the pommel of the saber.
To avenge it, the other hand, preparing itself,
Already reaches down, seeking to seize the steel <note>original: "le fer"; literally "the iron," a common poetic metonym for a sword.</note>;
But Engist’s blade, as quick as a lightning flash,
Comes to sever the head from the leaning neck.

Obald has halted before Harol.
This castle-lord <term>châtelain: a medieval lord who held or resided in a castle and exercised local jurisdiction; here used to describe a "robber baron" type</term>, a lover of plunder,

He falls swiftly upon beauty <note>This implies the abduction or assault of women in the territories he raids.</note>,
Ransoms the passing pilgrim from afar,
And even taxes the litany of the three holy days <note>Likely referring to the Rogation Days or the Easter Triduum; a way of illustrating Obald's extreme greed, as he demands payment even during sacred religious processions.</note>.

### CANTO VI.

He extends the tyranny of the manorial oven <term>four banal: a feudal monopoly where the lord forced all inhabitants to use his oven, charging a fee for the service</term>,
And shamelessly robs the merchant.
It was with regret that he had, for a moment,
Renounced his "useful" repose,
And that happiness so noble and so tranquil <note>The poet is being highly ironic here, describing Obald's life of robbery and exploitation as a "noble" and "tranquil" retirement.</note>.
Having been driven into the war, he wishes at least
To end it himself with a single blow,
And return to the keep <term>donjon: the innermost stronghold or main tower of a castle</term> that he loves.
The fighting would end with Harol;
Therefore, he swears Harol’s death,
And moves to strike; but beneath the arm he raises,
A blade immediately enters and sinks deep.
Suddenly he dies, and from the height of the battlements,
His ghost still insults his vassals.

Near the victor, whom he quietly threatens,
Then arrives the prudent Abunot.
He was watching for the moment—passing, repassing,
Turning, advancing, and retreating at once.
This young Englishman, who constantly evades capture,
Is dear to London, where his speed is celebrated.
There, he holds all the laurels of the race;
His hand seizes the swift gazelles,

And his heels, lighter than wings,

Rolls to his feet. "I give you thanks, Odin! <note>original: "Odin!" — Harol invokes the chief deity of Norse mythology, reflecting the poet's choice to blend medieval chivalry with pagan elements.</note>"
He speaks; then the runner flees in vain.
The wood thrown by this cautious warrior
Immediately breaks that leg so rapid.
Farewell the race and all future prizes.
What a lucky stroke for his obscure rivals!

But scarcely crowned with cypress <note>original: "cyprès" — In classical and European tradition, the cypress tree is a symbol of mourning and death.</note>,

O too vain hope of return!
Shields, lances, boar-spears <note>original: "épieux" — A heavy spear used primarily for hunting large game like boars, but also employed as a formidable infantry weapon.</note>,
Broken arrows litter the plain afar.
From the tangled and furious combatants
Confused cries rise to the heavens;
And victory between them is uncertain.

Before the altar, to the amorous Eloi
The beautiful Edgize had pledged her faith.
After twenty days of solitary bliss,
The surprised husband hears the roar of war.
He departs; farewell to quiet loves.
"Wait, wait," says the alarmed wife.
"Changing my dress, and armed for you alone,
I wish to follow you and watch over your days.
I will follow you, cease your vain talk."

### CANTO VI.

The slanting steel breaks through the shoulder,
Passes down to the lung, and brings death.
You, who fall without fanfare or glory,
And are covered now by humble turf—
Loving spouses—my verses at least
Shall teach your names to the <term>daughters of memory</term> <note>original: "filles de mémoire" — A poetic reference to the Muses of Greek mythology, the daughters of Zeus and Mnemosyne (the goddess of Memory).</note>.
Lodbrown triumphs; Engist steps toward him,
And the Dane’s arrogance bursts forth:
"Come die, come! I have promised my soldiers
A feast atop your noble <term>keep</term> <note>original: "donjon" — The central tower or innermost stronghold of a medieval castle.</note>;
But first, tearing the <term>coat of arms</term> <note>original: "écusson" — A heraldic shield displayed over a gateway to identify the noble family residing there.</note> from your gate,
I shall nail both your helmet and your head in its place."
Thus spoke this lover of banquets.
The men applaud him; and with his massive hands,
The proud giant heaves up an enormous stone.
From another side, Durthal—he of the misshapen brow—
Approaches, seeking an easy victory:
Cowardly and stealthy, he moves to strike the Englishman.
But his caution is timid and too slow.
Lodbrown follows the flight of his own hurled stone with his eyes;
His rival ducks to avoid it,
And the stone flies onward, striking down Durthal instead.

Lodbrown then spoke to the hero who threatened him:
“Very well, I wait; strike! To your vain audacity,
I wish to leave the honor of at least one blow.”
During these words and his mocking laughter,
His head was lowered too slowly:
The sharp spear, thrust with great force,
Breaks the helmet with its waving plume,
Splits the thickened vault of his hard skull,
And from the brain that it grazes as it flies through,
Both insolence and life suddenly depart. <note>The poet employs a classic epic trope here, where a character’s defining vice—in this case, Lodbrown's insolence—physically leaves the body along with his soul.</note>

Harol pursues and pierces Lévinsdal,
A renowned hunter, so fatal to foxes;
Then Thoril too, born noble and wise,
But who, by forever prolonging his feasts
And being slowly inflamed by wine,
In the end would heap insults upon his friends;
Then Vortimer, who without restraint or remorse,
In wagers, in the <term>glittering races</term> <note>original: "courses brillantes" — likely referring to horse racing or athletic competitions used for gambling.</note>,
And in the games of noisy taverns,
Squandered the fortunes of his children.
Fall, Athelbert, fall on those burning shores,
And leave your fearful <term>vassals</term> <note>original: "vassales" — the French specifically uses the feminine form, implying Athelbert was a predator or tyrant toward the women under his feudal authority.</note> in peace.

### CANTO VI.

Harol finally fulfills the wishes of Althun:
Rich, powerful, a lord over three cities,
A beloved husband, a father of obedient children,
Yet he was weary of this intrusive happiness. <note>The poet suggests that Harol, a man of action, finds the quiet life of a wealthy provincial lord to be stifling or "importunate."</note>
   The dark Alkent and his treacherous soldiers,
Emerging from the woods—traitors without danger—

Against the English they were supposed to protect.
Harol blushes and turns his eyes away:
Proud, loyal, and worthy of his glory,
He disdained such hateful assistance.
But he fights on, all too certain of victory.
Arthur appears, and Jule is beside him.
Alkent turns pale; their unexpected approach
Terrifies the sight of Rénisthal.
Come, brave Arthur, the noble support of Elfride.
Thus, while sailing toward distant Africa,

Soon it rises, and light, without a storm,
Flying alone through the azure plains,
It concentrates the fury of the <term>north winds</term> <note>original: "aquilons" — a classical poetic term for the cold, fierce winds from the north.</note>.

Under his banners, he secured victory.
Unshakable Engist was still fighting.
From afar, the robust Ladnor saw him.

There, he had fought hand-to-hand
And, being more skillful, had struck down at his feet...

Engist pulls it out and shudders with rage.
A second dart, suddenly launched,

# CANTO VI.

9.

While the tomb was entrusted with the heroes,
The skalds <term>skalds: Scandinavian poets and storytellers of the Viking Age who composed oral verses to commemorate the deeds of warriors and kings</term>, alone upon the neighboring rock,
Saluted them with new hymns.
Of noble Harol they tell the victory,
Naming thrice those children of glory
Whom this immortal combat has harvested,
To the great Odin <note>The chief god of the Norse pantheon, who receives the souls of brave warriors into Valhalla</note> they commend their shades <note>original: "ombres"; refers here to the souls or ghosts of the deceased</note>,
And, awakened within the dark caverns,
The echo responds to their solemn song.

### CANTO SEVEN.

Young Harol goes every day to console 
The sorrows of his beautiful captive. 
In this moment, he sees her more fearful; 
She groans and finds her tears once more. 
"Prince," she said, "held captive in this place, 
Do not add to my initial sorrows, 
And I may yet be able to forgive the fates. 
Spare me the sight of the cowardly Alkent. 
I met his love with contempt: 
Shall I now become the prize for his crime?" <note>original: "forfait"; refers to a wicked deed or a betrayal of honor</note>
Alkent arrives: "This woman is dear to me; 
Give her to me." — "But have you known how to please her?"

I should make her favors a duty for her? 
The imprudence of your desires leads you astray. 
Think first of deserving her choice,"

“Mine as well.” — “How?” — “Through feats of valor.

In this refusal, he sees an insult.
He obeys; but jealous suspicion
Already punishes his cowardly treason.

# CANTO VII.

He cannot endure Emma's severity:
Bitter tears flow down his face.
Albert had flattered himself in vain
With the hope of a comforting welcome; from the too-severe Blanche
He does not obtain even the favor of a glance.
Sad, he withdraws, and alone with his brother,
He cried out: “What a fatal change!
Is our imprudence not punished enough?
Believe me, Raoul, let us finally flee
The long tyranny of an unequal <note>original: "amour inégal"; referring to a love that is unrequited or where the social standing/affection is not balanced</note> love.
Why yield, why always tremble?
Our soul is consumed in vain sighs.
Let us seek peaceful loves elsewhere,
And forget the bitterness of these first ones.”
“— If you are able, my brother, I pity you.
But such an effort is not within your power.
The sweet inclination that causes our sorrows,
Which, despite ourselves, will shape our destinies,
Was born and grew during the games of childhood.
Constant love and honor: these are our laws.

### CANTO VII.

After two years of a peaceful reign,
Eginhal fell beneath the dagger.
For one month, the brow of young Eulhérie,
Whom his love sought in Neustria <note>A historical region in the northwestern part of the Frankish kingdom, often associated with the early French monarchy.</note>,
Had been adorned by his diadem <note>A crown or jeweled headband worn by monarchs.</note>.
The timid will of the magistrates
Placed the wise Elfride upon the throne in vain.
Obedient to the cries and the gold of the proud Dromont,
A vile populace has crowned this traitor,
Who was perhaps Eginhal’s assassin.
Then I fled. My Lord, fear this port:
Crime reigns there and promises you death.”
As these words were spoken, upon the silent shore
Raoul fixed a worried gaze.
Then suddenly: “The Danes are here.
I recognize their brilliant sterns,
The wider flanks of these vessels,
Their heavy masts enriched with emblems,
And the bird painted on the floating banners <note>original: "flammes"; in a naval context, these are long, narrow pennants or streamers flown from the mastheads of ships.</note>.
While we struggle against the winds,
In the dungeons, no doubt, innocence
Invokes the power of the English throne.”

And all in tears they rush into the vault.
Everyone shudders; everyone suddenly hears

Everywhere, a silent dread reigned.

> Proclaims Elfride, and as legitimate queen,
> Her sacred names resound three times.

Silent and sailing upon the waves,

Their long absence and a hostile fate

### CANTO VII.

Eric leaves behind the elite of his warriors,
And departs suddenly without fear or danger;
Into the fields they had come to protect,
He goes to scatter the Neustrian <note>original: "Neustriens." Neustria was the western part of the Frankish kingdom, often associated with the ancestors of the Normans.</note> band.
The fierce Althor to the brave men he commands
Still promises glorious battles:
Yet he flees; Oldar, following in his tracks,
Extended the devastation as far as the cities.
His vast camp overflows with plunder.
Young Harol <note>likely referring to a stylized version of Harold, often depicted in such epics with Danish heritage.</note> delays his successes;

In the future he saw her power.

### CANTO VIII.

In his lung, extinguishes his weak voice.
Lark and Ryslan, whose unruly intoxication
Strives to resist the sleep that weighs upon it,
Were surrounded by the ruins of the feast:
Raymond passes by, and stained all over with wine,
One of them sees him and cries: "Come closer, brother;
From this barrel let us drain the vast breast;
Let us strike, let us pillage, and let us drink to England." <note>original: "buvons l'Angleterre" (let us drink England). This is a figurative expression meaning to celebrate the conquest of the country through excessive drinking.</note>
Suddenly struck, these reckless men die,
Still clutching the cup between their teeth.
The harsh Calder, with his hollow and fierce eye,
Opens a deep mouth while snoring:
The entire blade <note>original: "sabre". In this historical/epic context, it refers to a heavy sword or falchion.</note> plunges into it; the Dane,
Stiffening, extends his trembling hand,
Utters a muffled cry, and vomits at once
His mead <term>mead: an alcoholic beverage made by fermenting honey with water, common in Norse culture</term> and his bloody soul.

Other warriors scattered before the tents,
Nameless heroes, whose wandering shadows

Their plaintive voice implores deaf gods;

### CANTO VIII.

They walk silently toward the captive beauty.
She was fleeing, pale and fearful;
Their cry of alarm rang out at once,
And the sword of the French falls upon them.
Brave Oldar, alerted by their flight,
Seizes his axe and rises, trembling.
From every side, the sudden rumor
Spreads, intensifies, and turns into an outcry.
On all sides, staggering drunkenness
Half-arms itself, longing for the poppies <note>A poetic reference to sleep or the daze of intoxication; poppies were historically associated with Morpheus, the god of dreams, and opium.</note>,
Runs aimlessly, raises a trembling hand,
And falls beneath the steel of the heroes.
The prisoners, who were freed without struggle
And are already moving off across the plain,
Hasten their steps toward the forest;
Dunstan and his worthy soldiers follow.
But the French, to ensure her <note>original: "sa fuite"; likely referring to the "captive beauty" mentioned in the first line.</note> escape,
Maintain a noble combat alone.
Marching to the right, they have thus
Diverted the enemy's pursuit toward themselves.
Weary at last, surrounded by spears,
And regretfully separated from one another,

# CANTO VIII.

They bow their heads, heavy with sorrow.

Where you fall asleep forevermore,

Of his attentive company:

### CANTO NINE.

The power of Wailte <note>Likely a stylized or archaic reference to a British kingdom or territory</note> was then expiring.
The brave Arthur having descended into the grave,
Left the land defenseless to the enemies,
And audacity had triumphed without danger.
The wise Olcan, too weakened by age,
His son barely in his fifteenth spring <gloss>his fifteenth year of life</gloss>,
And a few soldiers growing old far from the camps,
In vain opposed their courage against the numbers:
They fell beneath the iron <note>original: "le fer"—referring to the blades or swords</note> of the Danes.
Alkent, seated in the palace of kings,
Insults heaven; his unquenched rage,
Which still hunts the lives of the defeated,
Turns the city into a vast tomb.
Sunon and Sardat, accomplices in his crimes,
Go even to the altar to seize his victims.
The holy temples are surrendered to Crodo <note>A Germanic or Saxon deity, often depicted in early modern literature as a pagan idol of the northern tribes</note>.
But at last, the fleet of Raoul appears,

It was the moment when, already drawn together
Despite the effort of his numerous horde,
The two vessels, hooked by the gaff <note>A pole with a hook used to pull ships together or move heavy cargo</note>,

English, Wailtains, French—all must perish.”
Within the vessels seized by the boarding,
A horrible carnage continued.

Offer the scepter to the elder of these brothers,
Saying to him: "You have earned it;
And our choice is dictated by the people."
Raoul replies: "Wise Wailtains, fear the noble excess
Of your gratitude."

Burned my heart, my senses; I am conquered.
But know me: if through a base act
I must pay for marriage <term>hymen</term> <note>Derived from the name of the Greek god of marriage, this term is used poetically to refer to the wedding union itself.</note> and your tenderness;
If I must choose between honor and you,
My choice is made. — Listen. — Leave me.”

She sighs, and far from her, her brothers
Have descended onto the English shore.
They were crossing through solitary hamlets,
Both wavering in their vague projects.
London <note>original: "Londre"</note> loves them and its wish calls them back;
But to Raoul, whom she believes unfaithful,
The unjust Emma forbade return,
And this hero obeys love.
Passing near them, Jule <note>A companion or younger relative of the main heroes, often depicted as more emotionally sensitive.</note> stops at their voice.
His tears were flowing; mournfully he bows his head.
Albert said to him: “We know your misfortune.
Come, do not flee the friendship that consoles;
Open your ear to its sweet word,
And into its bosom pour out your grief.
Weep, and yet, against ills without remedy
Oppose, at last, the effort of your reason.

Perhaps abandoned to base treatment,
Perhaps without any care.... Go, worthy friends:
Justly for you, heaven blesses your weapons;
It prepares a happy future for you;
But I have a right to your remembrance,
And my misfortune asks you for tears."

### CANTO THE TENTH.

The sudden presence of the proud Althor
Astonishes Elfride, and informed her well enough.
Cold and severe, she listens in silence
To these words nobly spoken by him:
“We are all from a race of <note>original: "race des braves"—this refers to a lineage of heroes or warriors, emphasizing the aristocratic and martial values of the characters.</note> heroes.
I was camped upon a vast mountain,
And in the plain below, insolent and cheering,
I saw the slaves of dark <term>Odin—the chief deity in Norse mythology, often depicted here through a medieval Christian lens as a "dark" or pagan figure.</term>
Their shouts were an insult to us.
I fall upon them; lightning is not as fast.
Their front ranks break apart in terror,
And already their cowardly vanguard is in flight.
Then come their light squadrons,
Who suddenly scatter without a fight,
And easily seize control of my camp;
But their main battalions remain before me.
What matters a camp, a post, or a mountain?

Before <note>Oldar: Likely a Danish chieftain or enemy commander.</note> Oldar, my skillful retreat

During the night, while I sleep,

The smile of the generals wounds me;
“Go,” I said to them, “and fight without me.”
The reckless men fly to both sides.
To the bridge I run, and the attack is sudden;
There the <note>original: "brigands"—here used as a derogatory term for the Danish invaders.</note> brigands were advancing with great strides;
It was <note>Eric: Likely referring to a Danish chieftain or high-ranking warrior.</note> Eric and his numerous elite.
Already the dead were piling up upon the dead.
The darkness, the tumult, and the crowd
Led the fury of the combatants astray.
The bridge shakes, and it collapses into the waves.
Of this death <note>original: "trépas"—a poetic term for a violent or sudden death.</note>, I dread the horror.
A floating beam near me
Aids and supports my painful efforts.

But I repress a reckless audacity:
To perils that I did not approve,
To their fate which is undoubtedly fatal,
I abandoned my rebel soldiers.
The bridge lost, what does the rest matter to me?

Althor cries; “but one takes revenge on the coward;
Let us go to reinforce the <term>phalange (phalanx)—historically a tight-knit Greek infantry formation, but here used poetically to describe a disciplined body of troops.</term> phalanx of <note>Engist: Likely a reference to Hengist, the legendary Germanic leader who, according to tradition, led the first Anglo-Saxon settlement in Britain.</note> Engist.”

To valor, <note>Engist: Referring to Hengist, the legendary 5th-century Germanic leader. In this literary context, he is portrayed as a prudent and industrious military commander.</note> Engist joins prudence,

They were arriving; less greedy for glory,

A single thought occupies him: it is love.
He desires <note>original: "l'hymen"—While Hymen is the Greek god of marriage, the term is used here poetically to signify the marriage rites themselves.</note> marriage, but free and without alarm;

Rising, he says: “It is too much praying,
It is too much suffering, and too much self-humiliation.
What a long struggle! What turmoil in my soul!
Shameful turmoil! For whom? For a woman.”

“It is time, Harol, return to your senses.
Upon <term>laurels</term><note>original: "lauriers"—Symbolizing military victory and glory; the text suggests Harol should be ruling through conquest rather than being ruled by his heart.</note> you should receive the law!

When your refusals provoke my power,
To your charms I restore their liberty.
I was tranquil, happy, before Isaure...

When I first welcomed your fatal beauty!

Those eyes so sweet, that voice so touching,
That brow so pure, all that conquering charm;

He speaks again to the ungrateful woman he loves,
Alternately sitting, then walking with great strides,
And in his heart, the struggles are reborn.

Hardly listens, and answers at random;
Slowly and without aim, his distracted step moves forward;

Which signals his presence in the camp;
And sometimes from his silence escapes
The cherished name he would like to forget.
Even more sorrowful, he returns to his tent.

The stern honor, and constant pride.
Do not sing of love and its languors:
I hate love; it debases hearts.”

And let me be left to my deep thoughts.”

He hastens his chiefs, who are still slumbering,

And from his hand the uncertain weapon escapes.

Both were bold, yet prudent and skilled;
They opposed one another with invincible courage.
Toward the eyes, toward the heart, the sword-point approaches in vain;
Art <note>original: "l'art" — here referring to the "art of defense" or technical fencing skill.</note> knows how to foresee all that art meditates;
It grasps the true intent behind the false feints,
And reads the ruses of the hand within the eye;
To parried blows, the riposte is instantaneous;
New strikes fly like lightning;
Steel masters and always follows steel.
Weary, both of them can barely breathe.

The steed falls; a generous enemy,

“Go, worthy chief and valorous soldier,
Go; the hero is not always fortunate <note>original: "heureux" — in this context, it implies being favored by fortune or luck in battle</note>.”
Leaving Engist, he passes quickly;
And the vanquished whom his sword <note>original: "glaive" — a sword, often used in heroic poetry to evoke classical or medieval weaponry</note> threatens,
To escape, seek the deep wells,
The solitary tufts of shrubs,
The bushy meadows, the thick ferns,
And the height of the waving harvests <note>original: "moissons" — literally "harvests," referring here to fields of tall grain where soldiers might hide</note>.
Before Osla <note>A character pursuing the fleeing troops</note> runs the timid crowd;
But like the rapid bird of the tropics,
With its rosy beak and lily-white plumage,
Leaving the arid peak of its native rock,
Darts far away over the leveled waves;

<summary>During the battle, Engist's horse falls into a ditch, but his opponent Harol chivalrously spares his life. The defeated soldiers scatter into the landscape to hide from the pursuit of Osla.</summary>

Lively and brilliant, he pursues their flight <note>original: "sa vitesse" — literally "its speed," referring to the swift movement of the prey</note>,
And seizes them while they are lost in the air.

### CANTO ELEVEN.

Charles continued to follow the trail of Osla.
In the depths of a vast forest,
The reckless man wandered, slow and pensive.
There, the cunning of Crodo <note>Crodo was an ancient Saxon deity; in this context, he represents the pagan forces opposing the Christian knights</note> threatens him;
There, every Christian runs a double danger <note>This "double danger" likely refers to both physical peril and the spiritual threat of pagan enchantment</note>.
In this beautiful place where peace is fatal,
And which was enchanted by infernal power,
The valiant Roger was also wandering.
Raymond seeks a beloved mistress there;
And these Frenchmen arriving from Neustria <note>Neustria was the western part of the Frankish Empire, located in what is now northern France</note>,
Whom Eric had dispersed across the fields,
Driven by destiny toward this trap,
Are going to forget their distant homeland.
They walk separately beneath the shade.
Paulin is the first to find a wealthy village.
The fertile soil requires only gentle labor;
The rustic elegance of the scattered roofs,

And the cheerful abundance of the gardens,

This narrow enclosure, these vine-covered arbors,
These various fruits, and this modest retreat....
— They are yours, says a celestial voice.”
Surprised, he enters, and as a new owner,
Free and content in this humble domain,
With no memory of the distant war <note>The enchantment causes the knights to forget their military service and their homeland, Neustria</note>,

And pride is mingled with his gentleness.
To this power strengthened by wealth,
A jealous Paulin compares his own weakness.
Why, he asks, is there such inequality?
Why this unjust sharing of goods?
Deceptive rest! O vain liberty!

### CANTO XI.

“Obedience is still slavery.”
He sighed; and from his troubled heart
Fled that happiness he had barely tasted.
The voice said to him: “Heaven hears your complaint.
Leave this place; in the next enclosure
You shall be rich and a lord in your turn.”
So he walked toward this other dwelling;
And there, his fretful vanity smiled.
At the sight of him, the villager bows low.
Led pompously toward the keep <term>donjon</term> <note>In medieval architecture, the "donjon" or keep was the central, most secure tower of a castle, serving as the residence of the lord</note>,
He receives the oath of his vassals.
To confirm his new dignity,
A message from the suzerain <term>suzerain</term> <note>A feudal lord or baronial superior to whom other lords owed a degree of allegiance</note> summons him.
“What have I heard?” he then said; “What!
Always ranks and always homages?
Must I still suffer the law of a master?
Subjected himself to these shameful customs,
The suzerain groans just as I do.
Protecting voice, command, and let me be king.”
Charle <note>A reference to Charlemagne, the King of the Franks, whose knights are the protagonists of the poem</note>, further on, receives a diadem.
Surrounded by his many subjects,
Followed by the great nobles, elevated beneath the canopy,

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

Fresh and laughing, a young shepherdess

Grass and flowers compose her finery;
Over the contours whose form is so pure
A light veil is barely cast.
At this sight, the agitated Frenchman <note>Referring to Paulin, the Frankish knight and protagonist of this sequence</note>
Feels the rising flame of sweet desires.

It is the secret sanctuary of beauty.

A stranger with a rosy face,
With snowy breast, with a gaze both lively and tender,
And whose hand, which falls softly,
Seems to offer itself to a lover's kiss.
Her sweet silence is easy to understand.

### CANTO VI.

What a perilous moment for the Frenchman! <note>Referring to the knight Roger, a Frankish hero undergoing moral trials</note>
The beloved name that his lips repeat
Restores his strength, and prevents his defeat.
Ashamed, he departs without kissing that hand,
And sighs call back to him in vain.
He walked in silence through the forest.
Soon, a vast garden opens up before him.
With a light step, a young woman approaches.
Her grace is lively, and her smile is subtle;
The ebony of her hair falls and floats;
In her dark eyes, gaiety sparkles.
The linen <term>lin</term> <note>Fine linen fabric; in 18th-century poetry, "clear linen" was a common trope for garments that were tantalizingly semi-transparent</note> so sheer that veils her beauty,
And which the breath of the winds still stirs,
Leaves her charms exposed to view.
Young Roger <note>The text shifts to using this name, common in chivalric Romances like "Orlando Furioso," to emphasize the knight's role as a legendary hero resisting enchantment</note>, your constancy wavers.
“Follow me,” says this new lover.
“Perhaps at this very moment your Edgitha
Is listening to your rival’s vow.”
At the sacred name, Roger stops in confusion;
And mastering his unfaithful desires,
He flees once more, without turning his head;
But his triumph is mingled with sighs.

<summary>Roger (the Frenchman) faces a final temptation in an enchanted garden where a beautiful woman attempts to provoke his jealousy. By holding onto the name of his beloved Edgitha, he manages to overcome his desires and escape the garden's snares.</summary>

### THE ROSICRUCIANS,

Further on he sees a beauty, soft and slow:
In her blue eyes is a moist languor;
In her bearing is an indolent grace;
Her veiled voice reaches the heart;
Voluptuousness <term>volupté</term> <note>In French Romantic poetry, this term implies a refined, sensual pleasure that is both physical and spiritual</note> smiles as it ought;
Just like her, voluptuousness sighs;
Voluptuousness reddens her charming brow,
And quickens the movement of her breast.
Roger is troubled, and his constancy expires.
But his happiness lasted only a single day.
Born of pleasure, boredom <note>original: "ennui," here referring to a profound spiritual dissatisfaction or weariness that follows shallow gratification</note> avenged love.
"What!" he said, "always these sad fears,
Vain suspicions, oaths, and complaints?
Has feeling, then, nothing but tears?"
He departs; Crodo <note>A reference to a Germanic deity or magician often used in 18th-century literature to represent pagan sorcery</note>, prolonging his errors,
Leads him slowly back toward the garden.
Already defeated, the Frenchman had barely entered,
When the young girl <note>The lively, dark-haired woman described on the previous page</note> flew to his side.
Another happiness followed by another boredom <note>ennui</note>.
"Lively gaiety can seduce for a moment,"
The fickle Roger soon said to himself;
"But it signals a cold and light heart."

### CANTO XI.

In vain he searches for his straying reason.
There are the features of Emma, so beautiful and ever adored;
But, instructed by his heart,
He reaps the reward of his constancy;
Suddenly he escapes the sensual delights,
Sword in hand <note>original: *le fer en main*. Literally "the iron in hand," a standard poetic metonym for a blade or sword.</note>, he rushes to the idol and strikes;
The idol falls, and the spell is broken.
Altar, torches, and priestess—all vanish.
The wonders of the forest then cease.
Tender Emma, deceived by a long-lasting illusion <note>original: *prestige*. In the context of 18th and 19th-century French poetry, this refers to a magical deception or a phantom designed to trick the senses.</note>,
Was still fixing her eyes upon the necklace:
Suddenly it disappears, and far from her,
Her young friend feels this precious token <note>original: *gage*. A physical object, such as a piece of jewelry, serving as a pledge of love or a symbol of a vow.</note>
Reattach itself to his faithful breast.
All the Frenchmen whom the hero delivers,
At once astonished and punished for their weakness,
Are soon reunited at his side.
In battle, they burn with desire to follow him.
Charles <note>original: *Charle*. This is a poetic variation of the name Charles, referring here to one of Raoul's noble companions.</note> and Raymond, consoled and laughing,
Recounted to him their fall, of which he was unaware.
He hastens toward London; still more messengers
Come to speed his impatient steps.

# THE ROSICRUCIANS,

They said to him: “The English no longer have a refuge.
London falters, and sees beneath its ramparts
The bloody banners of the enemies.
The queen commands, and her voice is calm.
Eric and Oldar, at the gates of the city,
Have managed to cross the lists <note>original: *la lice*. In medieval fortification, this refers to the palisade or the space between the outer and inner walls of a fortress.</note> and the moat.
The first wall is already toppled.
These proud Danes, covered by a mobile roof <note>original: *toit mobile*. A reference to a siege engine, such as a gallery or penthouse, designed to protect attackers from projectiles as they approach the walls.</note>,
Sap the towers, and at the gates with heavy blows
Break the hinges and the wide bolts.
The long ladder is ready in their hands.
A well-aimed missile <note>original: *trait*. This term typically refers to an arrow, a crossbow bolt, or a javelin.</note> strikes down the warrior
Who dares to raise his head above the wall.
The shadow of night, the winds and the storm,
Do not stop this murderous assault.
The enemies, favored by fate,
With their fleet have astonished the Thames.
Its waters lead them to the foot of the walls.

Raoul, Albert, and their new troop,

He believed he saw all the perverse angels,
Black and howling, escaping from the underworld.
But these Danes, from the city gates,
Go to attack the surprised defenders.

Suddenly he falls, insulted by the barbarian <note>The poem uses "barbarian" to characterize the Viking/Danish invaders from the perspective of the besieged English.</note>,
Beside the mast where the hymn and the fanfare
Had thrice sung his name as victor. <note>This refers back to the archery competition mentioned on page 203, where the youth had previously won honors.</note>
Upon Egilan, Oldar now rushes.
The Englishman fights, incapable of flight.
A generous son, his recurring labors
And his tender care held onto life
An infirm father and a mother weakened
By toil, misery, and the passing years.
In this garden, he sometimes guides them,
Supporting their staggering and timid steps,
And spreading his cloak upon the stone,
He entrusts them to this makeshift seat;

## LES ROSECROIX,

Piety, lifting her hands to heaven,
Is still calm in her terror:
To the powerful God, her only recourse,
She abandons both her fate and her days. <note>The poet personifies "Piety" as a figure of quiet resignation and faith amidst the chaos of the battle.</note>
Humiliated by their recent defeat,
And reviving their warrior audacity,
In a combat that shall prove fatal to them,
Before the tower, Althor supports Oswal.
Harol appears; his inevitable blade <note>original: *fer* (iron). In epic poetry, "iron" is a common metonymy for a sword or weapon.</note>
Breaks the sword of the immobile Englishman
Who seemed unshakable upon his feet.
With a second blow, he splits his thick helmet.
Oswal turns pale, and weak though without a wound,
Losing his senses, he falls upon the threshold.
The incorrigible pride of his friend
Lets slip both threat and insult.
He was descending with an impetuous step
The winding stairs of the staircase;
He seeks to split the head of Harol, who is climbing;
By stepping aside, Harol deceives the blade
Which falls in vain and cuts only the air;
His strong hand, at the same time, stops...
