-># CANTO XII.<-

With his hand still extended and threatening;
Althor immediately rolls down the steps. <note>original: *degrés*. This refers to the winding staircase mentioned on the previous page.</note>
The Dane ascends surrounded by an escort,
And bursts through the door of the salon.
He expects to find the brave men of the court;
His scimitar <term>cimeterre: a curved sword, often used in romantic literature to denote a fierce or exotic warrior</term> sparkles and threatens;
Upon his youthful brow then shone audacity,
Fierce combats, victory, and love;
But what does he see? Fearless and defenseless,
The beautiful Emma, whom grief makes pale,
Shows the victor a serene face,
And reproaches herself for Raoul's absence;
At the sight of him, Blanche’s pride
Blushes and maintains an irritated silence;
Between the two, the noble and wise Elfride,
Effortlessly intrepid in misfortune,
Holds a dagger <term>poignard: a small, pointed blade; here, Elfride prepares for a "Roman death" to protect her honor</term> turned against her own breast;
Upon her, he fixes an astonished gaze;
But in the eyes of the charming Isaure
He sees love, reproach, and hope;
It is there that he reads and cherishes his duty;
It is there that he feels virtues he does not know.

"Faithful Erdal," he said, "let there be no delay;
Halt the valor of my soldiers.
In an instant I shall descend myself;

It shall command what honor ordains."

Eric and Oldar, followed by their brigands.
"What!" they say; "Harol, exceeding his rights,
Stops the Danes in their moment of triumph!
We, cowardly Erdal, we want riches,
London <note>original: *Londre*. The spelling reflects the French naming of the English capital.</note> to be pillaged, and the young princesses.
Brave friends, come, let us storm the tower:
Perhaps for Harol, his final day has dawned."

Here are Raoul and his valiant brother.
At the sight of them, the Englishman <note>original: *l'Anglais*. This likely refers to Raoul or the collective English resistance remaining in the city.</note> hopes once more,
And from his lips escapes a discreet cry.
The noisy anger of the two Danes
Turns toward this combat with regret.

Is about to pierce through Raoul's garment;
Only the sweet gift for which he paid so dearly <note>This refers to a romantic token or talisman given by a lady, a common trope in chivalric literature where a lover's gift provides miraculous protection in battle.</note>
Preserves his chest, which was but grazed;

He quickens and draws his blows closer;
A skillful blade quickly parries them.
The barbarian <note>original: *le barbare*. A typical epic convention where the narrator uses "barbarian" to describe the invading Danish forces.</note> grew pale with rage, all in vain.
Then, while retreating, his adversary
Lowers his arm and exposes his body;
Thus, cunning is joined to valor.

With his left hand, he diverts the point,
Which nonetheless tears that very hand,

Goes through that solitary muscle,
More delicate, more vital than the heart,
That blessed organ warned and tightened
By astonishment, joy, or grief. <note>The author provides a poetic anatomical description of the diaphragm or solar plexus, which in the 18th and 19th centuries was often considered a secondary "brain" or the true seat of sudden emotions.</note>

Bloody he falls, and bloody he returns;

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Charles and Raymond had also conquered;

At the foot of a tree, Osla catches her breath.
To the Danish camp spread along the shore
Raymond flies: under a nearby tent
He hears the sweet sound of a resonant lute;
Trembling with hope, he enters: "Dear Aldine!
— I am Aldin. <note>In French, *Aldine* is the feminine form and *Aldin* the masculine. This suggests a reveal of a character in disguise or a discovery of a lost companion.</note> — I love this double name."
Sad but proud, to Charles who advances,

One who knows how to die will never obey;
One who holds a blade <note>original: *fer* (iron). A common poetic term for a sword or weapon.</note> does not die without vengeance.
— Calm," he said, "this unjust wrath.
A trembling victor, I fall at your knees.
Yes, beauty must command the brave."

### CANTO XII.

She addresses the young Harol with a smile:
"True heroes are not conquerors.
Less enamored with a withered glory,
Be Christian, prince; be English.
Reign for me over the rich East Anglia <note>original: *Estanglie*. One of the traditional kingdoms of Anglo-Saxon England, located in the east of the island.</note>.
Its happy soil offers to diligent labor <note>original: *l’industrie*. In the poetic language of the time, this refers to the hard work and skill of farming rather than modern industrial factories.</note>
Lush meadows and thick shade;
Let your soldiers cultivate it in peace.
If there is for you a gift even more dear,
You shall obtain it from the tender Isaure."
Then, overcome, the generous Dane
Orders his warriors to retreat;
And the joyful cry of the English repeats:
"Long live the rose, the queen, and the cross! <note>The "rose" refers to the Rosicrucian knights, the "queen" to Elfride, and the "cross" to the Christian faith that now unites the former enemies.</note>"

# TO MR. FRANÇAIS,

->DIRECTOR GENERAL OF INDIRECT TAXES.<- <note>original: "Directeur Général des Droits réunis." This administrative body managed excise taxes in early 19th-century France.</note>

---

Far from the superb Pactolus, <term>Pactolus<gloss>A river in Lydia (modern-day Turkey) famed in mythology for its golden sands; here it represents the wealth and tax revenue managed by the dedicatee.</gloss></term>
Which rolls its gold before your eyes,
The Permessus <term>Permessus<gloss>A river in Greece flowing from Mount Helicon, sacred to the Muses; it serves here as a symbol of poetic and literary inspiration.</gloss></term> wanders through the grass,
A clear stream, yet without treasure.
But its banks have their own beauty,
And its waters are harmonious;
While your proud Pactolus
Never knew flowers nor the murmur of song.
For a moment, leave the Taxes aside,
And smile upon the Rosicrucians, <note>The Rosicrucians (Rose-Croix) were a legendary secret society of mystics and alchemists. The author is likely introducing a work with occult or satirical themes.</note>
You, an orator without empty words,
You, whose mind can grasp all things,
You, the upright man of our age,
To whom alone I owe my leisure.
While reading certain playful banter,
Which floats upon a certain river,
Certain people thought they should blush.

<summary>A poetic dedication to Antoine Français de Nantes, contrasting the cold world of state finance (the Pactolus) with the beauty of literature and the "Rosicrucian" themes of the book.</summary>

# SUMMARIES
## OF THE TWELVE CANTOS.

**Canto I.** The full court <term>Full court<gloss>In the medieval tradition, a "cour plénière" was a grand ceremonial assembly where a monarch wore their crown and gathered all the high nobility for feasts and governance.</gloss></term> of Elfride, widow of Chérébert <note>original: "Chérébert"; likely a reference to the Merovingian king Charibert I of Paris.</note>, King of Paris, and Queen of England. Emma and Blanche, daughters of Elfride and Chérébert. Dunstan, Engist, Oswal, and Althor, English lords. Raoul, his brother Albert, their sister Isaure, along with Charle, Roger, Jule, and Raymond, young French people attached to Elfride’s service. A sparrowhawk hunt. Alkent, Odon, and Rénisthal, English lords who remain faithful to the ancient Saxon religion. A falcon hunt. Jule and Olfide. A wild bull hunt. Mock battles. Rénisthal’s challenge to Jule.

**Canto II.** A feast in the palace. Raymond and Aldine. The arrival of Arthur, brother of Olcan who rules the Isle of Wight <note>original: "l’île de Wailte"; an archaic or phonetic French spelling of the Isle of Wight.</note>. The combat between Jule and Rénisthal. Arthur’s gift to the Queen. The Queen’s gift. The history of Agéline, mother of Olcan and Arthur. News of the approach and arrival of the Danes.

**Canto III.** The Danes on the English shore; the speech of their leader Harold. Fraull and Ghesler, the emissaries. The army divides into three divisions under the orders of Harold, Eric, and Oldar. Rudler and Nolk. The march of the Danes. Raymond and Aldine.

# SUMMARIES
## OF THE TWELVE CANTOS.

**Canto I.** The plenary court <note>A formal and ceremonial assembly of the ruler's vassals and high-ranking officials, typical of medieval courtly life.</note> of Elfrida, widow of Charibert <note>original: *Chérébert*; likely referring to Charibert I, a Merovingian King of Paris, though here placed in a fictionalized English context.</note>, King of Paris, and Queen of England. Emma and Blanche, daughters of Elfrida and Charibert. Dunstan, Engist, Oswal, and Althor, English lords. Raoul, his brother Albert, their sister Isaure, Charles, Roger, Julius, and Raymond, young Frenchmen attached to Elfrida’s service. A sparrowhawk hunt. Alkent, Odon, and Rénisthal, English lords faithful to the ancient Saxon religion <note>original: *culte*; referring to the pagan traditions of the Germanic Saxons before or during the transition to Christianity.</note>. A falcon hunt. Julius and Olfide. A wild bull hunt. Simulated combat. Rénisthal’s challenge to Julius.

**Canto II.** A banquet in the palace. Raymond and Aldine. The arrival of Arthur, brother of Olcan, who reigns on the Isle of Wight <note>original: *l’île de Wailte*; a phonetic French rendering of "Wight."</note>. The combat between Julius and Rénisthal. Arthur’s gift to the Queen. The Queen’s gift. The history of Ageline, mother of Olcan and Arthur. Announcements of the approach and arrival of the Danes.

**Canto III.** The Danes on the shores of England; a speech by their leader, Harold. Fraull and Ghesler, emissaries. The army divides into three divisions <note>original: *corps*; military units or divisions of an army.</note> under the command of Harold, Eric, and Oldar. Rudler and Nolk. The march of the Danes. Raymond and Aldine.

# TO MR. FRANÇAIS <note>Likely a proper name (e.g., François de Neufchâteau) or a personification of the French spirit/reader.</note>

> Their modesty <note>original: "pudeur"; implies a sense of bashfulness or moral propriety that the author feels he has challenged.</note> at the sharp language <note>original: "l’aigre langage"; refers to a biting, satirical, or overly bold tone in the verse.</note>
> Will no doubt soften.
> They wanted my muse <note>The personification of the author's creative inspiration.</note> to be more decorous; <note>original: "sage"; implies being well-behaved or conforming to conventional standards of "wisdom."</note>
> For them and for me, what a pity
> If wisdom is not pleasure!

---

<vocab>modesty, muse, wisdom, pleasure</vocab>

<summary>A brief poetic interlude in which the author defends his "sharp" literary style, arguing that true wisdom in poetry should not be divorced from pleasure and wit.</summary>
<keywords>poetry, modesty, muse, pleasure, wisdom, literary criticism, decorum</keywords>

# SUMMARIES
### OF THE TWELVE CANTOS.

**Canto First.** The plenary court <note>A formal full assembly of a monarch's court, usually held on major feast days.</note> of Elfride, widow of Chérébert <note>Likely Charibert I, a Merovingian King of Paris; the text blends French and English royal lineages.</note> king of Paris, and queen of England. Emma and Blanche, daughters of Elfride and Chérébert. Dunstan <note>Likely a reference to Saint Dunstan, the 10th-century Archbishop of Canterbury and advisor to kings.</note>, Engist, Oswal, Althor, English lords. Raoul, Albert his brother, their sister Isaure, Charle, Roger, Jule, Raymond, young Frenchmen attached to Elfride’s service. A hunt with the sparrowhawk. Alkent, Odon, Rénisthal, English lords, faithful to the ancient worship of the Saxons <note>Refers to the pre-Christian pagan beliefs of the Germanic settlers in England.</note>. A hunt with the falcon. Jule and Olfide. A hunt of the wild bull. Mock battles. Rénisthal’s challenge to Jule.

**Canto II.** A feast in the palace. Raymond and Aldine. Arrival of Arthur, brother of Olcan who reigns in the Isle of Wight <note>original: "l’île de Wailte"; an archaic or phonetic spelling of the island off the south coast of England.</note>. The combat between Jule and Rénisthal. Arthur’s gift to the queen. The queen’s gift. The story of Agéline, mother of Olcan and Arthur. Announcements of the approach and arrival of the Danes.

**Canto III.** The Danes on the shores of England; a speech by their leader Harol. Fraull and Ghesler, emissaries. The army divides into three divisions <note>original: "corps"</note>, under the orders of Harol, Eric, and Oldar. Rudler and Nolk. The march of the Danes. Raymond and Aldine.

<summary>This page serves as a table of contents for an epic poem, outlining the initial chapters which establish a royal court in England and the impending threat of a Danish invasion.</summary>
<keywords>Elfride, Chérébert, Dunstan, Saxons, Danes, Harol, Arthur, Jule, Rénisthal, England, Isle of Wight, chivalry, epic poetry</keywords>

# SUMMARIES.

Jule and Olfide. Weapons, war attire. Elfride’s speech to her barons; institution of the Rosicrucians <note>original: "Rosecroix"; refers to a legendary spiritual and cultural movement, here presented as a chivalric order established for defense.</note>; arrangements for the defense.

**Canto IV.** The generals report to their posts. Dunstan goes to raise new troops. The young warriors seek glorious adventures. The princesses give scarves <note>These scarves or sashes were often given as tokens of favor in chivalric traditions.</note> to their pages. Fraull and Ghesler. Charle and Osla. Roger. Raymond and Aldine—the latter under the name Aldin—are taken prisoner and led into Oldar’s camp; Aldine’s song and prayer; the story of the love of Almon and Elidda; Raymond and Aldine escape. Raoul and Albert send the queen flags captured from the enemy and are named knights-of-honor to the princesses.

**Canto V.** Arthur marches toward Engist’s camp; he is joined by Jule. The scheme of Alkent and his friends. Althor fights Oldar’s army. The pilgrimage of the two princesses; they are surprised by the Danes and rescued by Raoul and Albert; Emma’s gift to Raoul. The kidnapping of Isaure; her brothers pursue the kidnappers. Charle and Osla. Combat between the troops of Oswal and those of Eric.

**Canto VI.** Isaure is brought before Harol; this prince’s speech. Isaure’s regrets. Elfride’s fears. Battle between Harol’s army and Engist’s. Treason of Alkent and his friends. Arrival of Arthur and Jule; death of Odon; Jule

<meta>This page continues the summaries of the twelve cantos of the epic poem, picking up in the middle of the summary for Canto VI. It details the military campaigns of the English and their French allies against the Danish invaders, as well as the personal trials of the protagonists Raoul, Albert, and the captured princess Isaure.</meta>

# SUMMARIES.

...learns of Olfide’s fate and punishes Rénisthal; the exploits and death of Arthur. Engist is wounded; Harol is victorious.

**Canto VII.** Alkent asks Prince Harol for Isaure’s hand; he <gloss>Alkent</gloss> meets with a refusal and departs for the conquest of the Isle of Wight <note>original: "l’île de Wailte"; the author uses an archaic or phonetic spelling for the island off the south coast of England.</note>. Raoul and Albert return to London; Emma, deceived by a false account, forbids Raoul from appearing before her again; by order of the queen, the two brothers set sail and go to the aid of the people of Wight. Contrary winds drive them toward Guernsey <note>original: "Guerzel"; likely referring to the Channel Island.</note>; unrest in this island; the Danes seize it; they are defeated by Raoul and Albert; the island passes under the rule of Elfride. The positions of the armies of Eric, Oldar, and Harol. This prince refuses to hand Isaure over to the queen’s ambassador, but grants freedom to the other captives.

**Canto VIII.** Charle, Roger, and Raymond rescue some female captives. Dunstan marches at the head of a large troop and a rich convoy; he burns his own castle; he escapes the Danes, but his troop and the convoy fall into their hands; during the night, he enters their camp, accompanied by the three Frenchmen, and delivers the prisoners. The funeral procession of Prince Arthur.

**Canto IX.** Raoul and Albert on the Isle of Wight; defeat of the Danes; death of Alkent and his accomplices; Raoul refuses the crown and has it given to the young Emma. A conversation between Harol and Isaure.

<meta>This page concludes the summaries of the twelve cantos of the epic poem, picking up at the end of Canto IX. It covers the final military engagements, the supernatural challenge of an enchanted forest, the siege of London, and the resolution of the main characters' arcs.</meta>

# SUMMARIES.

Julia’s <note>original: "Jule"; a character name.</note> sorrow; the wretched state of Olfide. Aldine rescues Raymond and other prisoners. The story of Caldor and Esline; Caldor is killed by Roger.

**Canto X.** Defeat of the armies of Althor and Oswal. Harol sends Isaure back; his regrets; he attacks Engist’s army; the exploits of Osla; the English are defeated.

**Canto XI.** The enchanted forest; Raoul destroys the enchantment and marches toward London <note>original: "Londre"</note> at the head of the warriors he has freed. The Danes are beneath the city walls. Deaths of Olfide and Julia.

**Canto XII.** London is taken by storm; the brilliant valor of Harol. Outside the city, Charles <note>original: "Charle"</note> and Raymond fight Osla’s troop. Harol’s humanity; the fury of Eric and Oldar; Roger, Dunstan, Engist, Oswal, Althor; Harol forces entry into the tower; Emma, Blanche, Elfride, and Isaure; Harol has the carnage suspended; Eric and Oldar refuse to obey; Raoul and Albert arrive, fight, and kill these two leaders. Outside the city, the Danes are defeated; Raymond is reunited with Aldine; Charles and Osla. The fate of Raoul, Albert, and Harol.
